People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

I’m here because my sleep has gotten… not great, I suppose. I’m normally very disciplined about it, I have a routine and I function well, but since my cousin died, I keep lying awake replaying that night.

And then I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with these bits of hospital images in my head. I can still get myself to work, but I’m going into board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces, if that makes sense.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not exactly like I’m back there, no… or at least I don’t think I’d describe it that strongly.

It’s more that at odd moments—if a phone rings late, or headlights glare on the highway—I suddenly get this very sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer’s voice again in my head. It sort of pushes in before I can stop it, and I have to pause and steady myself for a moment.

Does that count as reliving it, or is that different from what you mean?

Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or reminders of your cousin's death that you try to push away or avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve been deliberately avoiding them, exactly. I mean… I don’t sit and dwell on it if I can help it, but I think that’s fairly normal, isn’t it?\n\nThere are moments I’d rather move past quickly, yes, but I haven’t noticed myself making a big effort to push specific memories away. If there’s something in particular you mean by avoiding, I can try to think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were reliving the night of your cousin's death, as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I’m back there, no… or at least I don’t think I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nIt’s more that at odd moments—if a phone rings late, or headlights glare on the highway—I suddenly get this very sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer’s voice again in my head. It sort of pushes in before I can stop it, and I have to pause and steady myself for a moment.\n\nDoes that count as reliving it, or is that different from what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body watching yourself when these intrusive images or memories happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I wouldn’t say I feel outside my body or like I’m watching myself from somewhere else.\n\nIt’s more that I get startled or pulled into the memory for a moment, and then I try to collect myself. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident occur?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose anxious is fair, though I wouldn’t say it’s overwhelming every time. It passes, but it does make me feel… shaken, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or reminders of your cousin's death that you try to push away or avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve been deliberately avoiding them, exactly. I mean… I don’t sit and dwell on it if I can help it, but I think that’s fairly normal, isn’t it?\n\nThere are moments I’d rather move past quickly, yes, but I haven’t noticed myself making a big effort to push specific memories away. If there’s something in particular you mean by avoiding, I can try to think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were reliving the night of your cousin's death, as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I’m back there, no… or at least I don’t think I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nIt’s more that at odd moments—if a phone rings late, or headlights glare on the highway—I suddenly get this very sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer’s voice again in my head. It sort of pushes in before I can stop it, and I have to pause and steady myself for a moment.\n\nDoes that count as reliving it, or is that different from what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body watching yourself when these intrusive images or memories happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I wouldn’t say I feel outside my body or like I’m watching myself from somewhere else.\n\nIt’s more that I get startled or pulled into the memory for a moment, and then I try to collect myself. But I’m still aware I’m here, if that makes sense. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are there any people, places, or activities that you've been avoiding because they remind you of your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I haven’t stopped seeing people or going places because of it, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI suppose there are little moments where something feels uncomfortable and I move through it quickly, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding whole activities or places. If I were doing that, I think I’d recognize it… I hope I would.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not constantly, thankfully. Maybe a few times in a day on a bad day, and then some days only once or not really at all.

It tends to be at odd moments — like if the phone rings late, or I’m on the highway and the headlights glare in a certain way. Then I’ll get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I’ll hear the police officer’s voice in my mind, and I have to pause for a moment to steady myself.

I can still get myself to work, but I’m going into board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think guilt is the main thing, no. I mean, I’ve gone over the details in my head, of course, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I’m not sitting here thinking it was my fault, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Not behaviors, really… I don’t think I’m doing anything repetitive in that sense. I suppose… yes, in a way. It’s not so much hobbies, exactly. I’ve still gone to the prayer gatherings, and I’ve tried to do what’s expected.

I think… maybe they’ve eased a little? Or at least I’m getting better at settling myself when they happen.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because my sleep has gotten… not great, I suppose. I’m normally very disciplined about it, I have a routine and I function well, but since my cousin died, I keep lying awake replaying that night.\n\nAnd then I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with these bits of hospital images in my head. I can still get myself to work, but I’m going into board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think guilt is the main thing, no. I mean, I’ve gone over the details in my head, of course, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I’m not sitting here thinking it was my fault, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Not behaviors, really… I don’t think I’m doing anything repetitive in that sense. Thoughts, yes, a bit. It’s not constant, but at odd moments—like if a phone rings late, or I see headlights glaring on the highway—I’ll suddenly get this very sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or hear the police officer’s voice in my head. It sort of pushes in before I can stop it, and I have to pause and steady myself. I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I haven’t stopped going to work or seeing people. I’m trying to keep things normal, really.\n\nThere may be small things I don’t even notice, but I wouldn’t say I’m actively avoiding places or people. Is that… is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Has your sleep disturbance or these intrusive images been causing you significant distress or interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still managing the important things. I’m showing up for the essential meetings and keeping things moving, for the most part.\n\nBut I have had to hand off some community commitments, and I’ve been cutting my workdays shorter than usual. I’m just not as steady with people as I normally am, or as effective, I suppose. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it is affecting me some.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these sleep disturbances and intrusive images?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now, I think. It started that night—when my cousin died in the highway collision. Since then the sleep and those images have been coming up… not every minute, but enough that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident occur?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose anxious is fair, though I wouldn’t say it’s overwhelming every time. It’s more that, at odd moments, something sets it off—like a phone ringing late, or headlights glaring on the highway—and then I get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer’s voice in my head.\n\nI usually just stop for a moment and steady myself. It passes, but it does make me feel… shaken, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or reminders of your cousin's death that you try to push away or avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve been deliberately avoiding them, exactly. If there’s something in particular you mean by avoiding, I can try to think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were reliving the night of your cousin's death, as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I’m back there, no… or at least I don’t think I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nIt’s more that at odd moments—if a phone rings late, or headlights glare on the highway—I suddenly get this very sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer’s voice again in my head. If I were doing that, I think I’d recognize it… I hope I would.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident occur?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s… unsettling more than anything. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because my sleep has gotten… not great, I suppose. I can still get myself to work, but I’m going into board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think guilt is the main thing, no. I mean, I’ve gone over the details in my head, of course, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I’m not sitting here thinking it was my fault, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Not behaviors, really… I don’t think I’m doing anything repetitive in that sense. Thoughts, yes, a bit. It’s not constant, but at odd moments—like if a phone rings late, or I see headlights glaring on the highway—I’ll suddenly get this very sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or hear the police officer’s voice in my head. It sort of pushes in before I can stop it, and I have to pause and steady myself. I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I haven’t stopped going to work or seeing people. I’m trying to keep things normal, really.\n\nThere may be small things I don’t even notice, but I wouldn’t say I’m actively avoiding places or people. Is that… is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Has your sleep disturbance or these intrusive images been causing you significant distress or interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still managing the important things. I’m showing up for the essential meetings and keeping things moving, for the most part.\n\nBut I have had to hand off some community commitments, and I’ve been cutting my workdays shorter than usual. I’m just not as steady with people as I normally am, or as effective, I suppose. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it is affecting me some.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these sleep disturbances and intrusive images?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now, I think. It started that night—when my cousin died in the highway collision. Since then the sleep and those images have been coming up… not every minute, but enough that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident occur?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose anxious is fair, though I wouldn’t say it’s overwhelming every time. It’s more that, at odd moments, something sets it off—like a phone ringing late, or headlights glaring on the highway—and then I get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer’s voice in my head.\n\nI usually just stop for a moment and steady myself. It passes, but it does make me feel… shaken, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or reminders of your cousin's death that you try to push away or avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve been deliberately avoiding them, exactly. I mean… I don’t sit and dwell on it if I can help it, but I think that’s fairly normal, isn’t it?\n\nThere are moments I’d rather move past quickly, yes, but I haven’t noticed myself making a big effort to push specific memories away. If there’s something in particular you mean by avoiding, I can try to think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were reliving the night of your cousin's death, as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I’m back there, no… or at least I don’t think I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nIt’s more that at odd moments—if a phone rings late, or headlights glare on the highway—I suddenly get this very sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer’s voice again in my head. It sort of pushes in before I can stop it, and I have to pause and steady myself for a moment.\n\nDoes that count as reliving it, or is that different from what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body watching yourself when these intrusive images or memories happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I wouldn’t say I feel outside my body or like I’m watching myself from somewhere else.\n\nIt’s more that I get startled or pulled into the memory for a moment, and then I try to collect myself. But I’m still aware I’m here, if that makes sense. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are there any people, places, or activities that you've been avoiding because they remind you of your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I haven’t stopped seeing people or going places because of it, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI suppose there are little moments where something feels uncomfortable and I move through it quickly, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding whole activities or places. If I were doing that, I think I’d recognize it… I hope I would.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident occur?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s… unsettling more than anything. I don’t completely fall apart, but when it happens it can really catch me off guard.\n\nLike if the phone rings late or I’m driving and the headlights glare a certain way, I’ll suddenly see the hospital corridor again, or hear the police officer’s voice in my head. I’d say… a little better than at the very beginning, maybe, but not back to normal.

I’m usually quite disciplined about sleep, so it’s been unsettling. Since my cousin died, I’ll lie there replaying that night, and then I’ll wake around three or four with little scraps of hospital images in my mind. I still get up and go to work, but some mornings I go into board meetings feeling as if I’ve only slept in pieces.

So… not worse, I don’t think. Maybe slightly easing, but still there more than I’d like. Is that the kind of change you mean?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because my sleep has gotten… not great, I suppose. I’m normally very disciplined about it, I have a routine and I function well, but since my cousin died, I keep lying awake replaying that night.\n\nAnd then I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with these bits of hospital images in my head. I can still get myself to work, but I’m going into board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think guilt is the main thing, no. I mean, I’ve gone over the details in my head, of course, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I’m not sitting here thinking it was my fault, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Not behaviors, really… I don’t think I’m doing anything repetitive in that sense. Thoughts, yes, a bit. It’s not constant, but at odd moments—like if a phone rings late, or I see headlights glaring on the highway—I’ll suddenly get this very sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or hear the police officer’s voice in my head. It sort of pushes in before I can stop it, and I have to pause and steady myself. I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I haven’t stopped going to work or seeing people. I’m trying to keep things normal, really.\n\nThere may be small things I don’t even notice, but I wouldn’t say I’m actively avoiding places or people. Is that… is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Has your sleep disturbance or these intrusive images been causing you significant distress or interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still managing the important things. I’m showing up for the essential meetings and keeping things moving, for the most part.\n\nBut I have had to hand off some community commitments, and I’ve been cutting my workdays shorter than usual. I’m just not as steady with people as I normally am, or as effective, I suppose. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it is affecting me some.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these sleep disturbances and intrusive images?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now, I think. It started that night—when my cousin died in the highway collision. Since then the sleep and those images have been coming up… not every minute, but enough that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident occur?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose anxious is fair, though I wouldn’t say it’s overwhelming every time. It’s more that, at odd moments, something sets it off—like a phone ringing late, or headlights glaring on the highway—and then I get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer’s voice in my head.\n\nI usually just stop for a moment and steady myself. It passes, but it does make me feel… shaken, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or reminders of your cousin's death that you try to push away or avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve been deliberately avoiding them, exactly. I mean… I don’t sit and dwell on it if I can help it, but I think that’s fairly normal, isn’t it?\n\nThere are moments I’d rather move past quickly, yes, but I haven’t noticed myself making a big effort to push specific memories away. If there’s something in particular you mean by avoiding, I can try to think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were reliving the night of your cousin's death, as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I’m back there, no… or at least I don’t think I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nIt’s more that at odd moments—if a phone rings late, or headlights glare on the highway—I suddenly get this very sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer’s voice again in my head. It sort of pushes in before I can stop it, and I have to pause and steady myself for a moment.\n\nDoes that count as reliving it, or is that different from what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body watching yourself when these intrusive images or memories happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I wouldn’t say I feel outside my body or like I’m watching myself from somewhere else.\n\nIt’s more that I get startled or pulled into the memory for a moment, and then I try to collect myself. But I’m still aware I’m here, if that makes sense. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are there any people, places, or activities that you've been avoiding because they remind you of your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I haven’t stopped seeing people or going places because of it, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI suppose there are little moments where something feels uncomfortable and I move through it quickly, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding whole activities or places. If I were doing that, I think I’d recognize it… I hope I would.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident occur?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s… unsettling more than anything. I don’t completely fall apart, but when it happens it can really catch me off guard.\n\nLike if the phone rings late or I’m driving and the headlights glare a certain way, I’ll suddenly see the hospital corridor again, or hear the police officer’s voice in my head. It’s sharp enough that I have to stop for a second and steady myself, but then I try to get back to what I was doing.\n\nSo I suppose the distress is there, but maybe not overwhelming? I’m not sure if that’s the right way to describe it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident occur in a typical day?\n\nPatient: Not constantly, thankfully. Maybe a few times in a day on a bad day, and then some days only once or not really at all.\n\nIt tends to be at odd moments — like if the phone rings late, or I’m on the highway and the headlights glare in a certain way. Then I’ll get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I’ll hear the police officer’s voice in my mind, and I have to pause for a moment to steady myself.\n\nBut it’s not all day long. I’m still functioning. Is that the kind of frequency you mean?\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been experiencing these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It started that night, after my cousin died in the highway collision.\n\nI mean, it hasn’t been every single moment since then, but those images have been coming up on and off for about three weeks now. Is that the kind of timeframe you’re asking for?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific things you used to enjoy doing before your cousin's death that you haven't felt like doing since then?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. But emotionally it felt like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nI don’t know if that answers your question, but that’s the closest thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in the frequency or intensity of these intrusive images over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe they’ve eased a little? Or at least I’m getting better at settling myself when they happen.\n\nIt’s still at those odd moments — if the phone rings late, or I’m driving and the headlights hit in that glaring way, I’ll suddenly see the hospital corridor very sharply, or hear the police officer’s voice in my head. Is that what you mean, or are you asking about more specific situations?\n\nDoctor: Have your sleep disturbances and intrusive images been getting better or worse over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’d say… a little better than at the very beginning, maybe, but not back to normal.\n\nI’m usually quite disciplined about sleep, so it’s been unsettling. I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I haven’t stopped going to work or seeing people. I’m trying to keep things normal, really.\n\nThere may be small things I don’t even notice, but I wouldn’t say I’m actively avoiding places or people. Is that… is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Has your sleep disturbance or these intrusive images been causing you significant distress or interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still managing the important things. I’m showing up for the essential meetings and keeping things moving, for the most part.\n\nBut I have had to hand off some community commitments, and I’ve been cutting my workdays shorter than usual. I’m just not as steady with people as I normally am, or as effective, I suppose. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it is affecting me some.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these sleep disturbances and intrusive images?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now, I think. It started that night—when my cousin died in the highway collision. Since then the sleep and those images have been coming up… not every minute, but enough that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident occur?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose anxious is fair, though I wouldn’t say it’s overwhelming every time. It’s more that, at odd moments, something sets it off—like a phone ringing late, or headlights glaring on the highway—and then I get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer’s voice in my head.\n\nI usually just stop for a moment and steady myself. It passes, but it does make me feel… shaken, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or reminders of your cousin's death that you try to push away or avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve been deliberately avoiding them, exactly. I mean… I don’t sit and dwell on it if I can help it, but I think that’s fairly normal, isn’t it?\n\nThere are moments I’d rather move past quickly, yes, but I haven’t noticed myself making a big effort to push specific memories away. If there’s something in particular you mean by avoiding, I can try to think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were reliving the night of your cousin's death, as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I’m back there, no… or at least I don’t think I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nIt’s more that at odd moments—if a phone rings late, or headlights glare on the highway—I suddenly get this very sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I hear the police officer’s voice again in my head. It sort of pushes in before I can stop it, and I have to pause and steady myself for a moment.\n\nDoes that count as reliving it, or is that different from what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body watching yourself when these intrusive images or memories happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I wouldn’t say I feel outside my body or like I’m watching myself from somewhere else.\n\nIt’s more that I get startled or pulled into the memory for a moment, and then I try to collect myself. But I’m still aware I’m here, if that makes sense. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are there any people, places, or activities that you've been avoiding because they remind you of your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I haven’t stopped seeing people or going places because of it, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI suppose there are little moments where something feels uncomfortable and I move through it quickly, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding whole activities or places. If I were doing that, I think I’d recognize it… I hope I would.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident occur?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s… unsettling more than anything. I don’t completely fall apart, but when it happens it can really catch me off guard.\n\nLike if the phone rings late or I’m driving and the headlights glare a certain way, I’ll suddenly see the hospital corridor again, or hear the police officer’s voice in my head. It’s sharp enough that I have to stop for a second and steady myself, but then I try to get back to what I was doing.\n\nSo I suppose the distress is there, but maybe not overwhelming? I’m not sure if that’s the right way to describe it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident occur in a typical day?\n\nPatient: Not constantly, thankfully. Maybe a few times in a day on a bad day, and then some days only once or not really at all.\n\nIt tends to be at odd moments — like if the phone rings late, or I’m on the highway and the headlights glare in a certain way. Then I’ll get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, or I’ll hear the police officer’s voice in my mind, and I have to pause for a moment to steady myself.\n\nBut it’s not all day long. I’m still functioning. Is that the kind of frequency you mean?\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been experiencing these intrusive images of the hospital or the accident?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It started that night, after my cousin died in the highway collision.\n\nI mean, it hasn’t been every single moment since then, but those images have been coming up on and off for about three weeks now. Is that the kind of timeframe you’re asking for?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific things you used to enjoy doing before your cousin's death that you haven't felt like doing since then?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. It’s not so much hobbies, exactly. I’ve still gone to the prayer gatherings, and I’ve tried to do what’s expected.\n\nBut even there, where I normally would feel comforted, I just… didn’t feel much warmth or relief inside. My nieces were trying to comfort me, and I was grateful, of course, and I behaved properly, I think. But emotionally it felt like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nI don’t know if that answers your question, but that’s the closest thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in the frequency or intensity of these intrusive images over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe they’ve eased a little? Or at least I’m getting better at settling myself when they happen.\n\nIt’s still at those odd moments — if the phone rings late, or I’m driving and the headlights hit in that glaring way, I’ll suddenly see the hospital corridor very sharply, or hear the police officer’s voice in my head. It just pushes in for a moment, and I have to pause and steady myself.\n\nBut I don’t want to overstate it. It’s not all the time, and I’m managing. Does that answer what you mean by frequency and intensity?\n\nDoctor: Are there specific things or stimuli that remind you of your cousin's death that you now find yourself trying to avoid or escape?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding things exactly. I mean, there are moments when something reminds me, and I’d rather not sit with it, of course… but I don’t think I’ve been deliberately escaping anything in a major way.\n\nMaybe I just try to keep myself composed and move on when it comes up. Is that what you mean, or are you asking about more specific situations?\n\nDoctor: Have your sleep disturbances and intrusive images been getting better or worse over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’d say… a little better than at the very beginning, maybe, but not back to normal.\n\nI’m usually quite disciplined about sleep, so it’s been unsettling. Since my cousin died, I’ll lie there replaying that night, and then I’ll wake around three or four with little scraps of hospital images in my mind. I still get up and go to work, but some mornings I go into board meetings feeling as if I’ve only slept in pieces.\n\nSo… not worse, I don’t think. Maybe slightly easing, but still there more than I’d like. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your sleep disturbances and intrusive images been present for less than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes — definitely less than six months. It’s been about three weeks, really, since the night my cousin died in the highway collision.\n\nI know that’s not very long in the scheme of things… but that’s the timeframe. Is that what you were asking?"
